========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I do. Not every time ends with that, if that’s what you mean.

Sometimes if the kitchen’s empty—after a church supper, for instance, when I’ve brought things home—I’ll start with just “putting away” leftovers, and then somehow I’ve eaten crackers and cheese, biscuits, bits of whatever I baked for book club… all in one go. Far more than I’d ever put on a plate and call a proper meal.

It’s strange, because I’m quite fussy about presentation normally—at work I’m measuring frames and noticing tiny details, and at home I’ll arrange things nicely if people are coming over—but when it happens it’s just standing there in the kitchen, picking at things until I realize how much has gone.

My daughter has told me I look too thin, and there’s a woman at church who said something similar—she meant it kindly, I think, though I ended up thinking about it all through the service instead of listening properly.\n\nBut even with that, if my skirt feels the slightest bit tight, or the scale is up even a pound, I just panic. It’s like my head tells me I’ve somehow become huge overnight, even though part of me knows that can’t be right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. Not every time ends with that, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes if the kitchen’s empty—after a church supper, for instance, when I’ve brought things home—I’ll start with just “putting away” leftovers, and then somehow I’ve eaten crackers and cheese, biscuits, bits of whatever I baked for book club… all in one go. Far more than I’d ever put on a plate and call a proper meal.\n\nIt’s strange, because I’m quite fussy about presentation normally—at work I’m measuring frames and noticing tiny details, and at home I’ll arrange things nicely if people are coming over—but when it happens it’s just standing there in the kitchen, picking at things until I realize how much has gone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Since last winter, really… I suppose there’s hardly been a week without it happening at least once.

It’s usually private, when no one’s about. I’ll have one of those spells where I eat far too much in a short time, and then I try to make up for it—sometimes by being sick, sometimes by not eating properly afterward, or by walking and walking more than I meant to. I don’t like admitting that.

Since last winter, I think… there’s hardly been a week when I haven’t had at least one of those private spells. Not always exactly the same, you know—sometimes I tell myself I’m just tidying up food, the way my mother used to hate waste, and then it’s suddenly become something else.

I’m not sure I can separate it out neatly, if I’m honest. In my head, the eating and the “making up for it” sort of belong together, even if it doesn’t always happen in the same way.

There may be times I’ve eaten too much and then just gone to bed or tried to carry on, but I don’t keep count. I’m sorry—my mind goes straight to whether I did something afterward, and then I get muddled. It’s like when I’m at work fitting someone’s frames and they ask one simple question, and suddenly I’m thinking about the whole shape of their face and the lighting in the shop… I lose the exact bit I was meant to answer.

So… I don’t really know how often.

I wish I could say it was about feeling uncomfortable or wanting to be “healthy,” but really it’s the weight.\n\nEven when my daughter says I look too thin, or that woman from church mentioned it—she’s always so neat herself, you know, lovely hats on Sundays—still, if my skirt feels snug or the scale is up even a pound, I just… panic. It feels like I’ve suddenly let myself become huge overnight, even though some sensible part of me knows that can’t be true.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have these episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, really… I suppose there’s hardly been a week without it happening at least once.\n\nIt’s usually private, when no one’s about. I’ll have one of those spells where I eat far too much in a short time, and then I try to make up for it—sometimes by being sick, sometimes by not eating properly afterward, or by walking and walking more than I meant to. I don’t like admitting that.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have these episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without then making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, I think… there’s hardly been a week when I haven’t had at least one of those private spells. Not always exactly the same, you know—sometimes I tell myself I’m just tidying up food, the way my mother used to hate waste, and then it’s suddenly become something else.\n\nAnd afterward I usually try to make up for it somehow—being sick, or not eating properly, or walking far too much. I know you asked about without making myself throw up… but it all rather gets tangled together in my head.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to undo it by making yourself throw up, skipping meals, or excessive exercise?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out neatly, if I’m honest. In my head, the eating and the “making up for it” sort of belong together, even if it doesn’t always happen in the same way.\n\nThere may be times I’ve eaten too much and then just gone to bed or tried to carry on, but I don’t keep count. I’m sorry—my mind goes straight to whether I did something afterward, and then I get muddled. It’s like when I’m at work fitting someone’s frames and they ask one simple question, and suddenly I’m thinking about the whole shape of their face and the lighting in the shop… I lose the exact bit I was meant to answer.\n\nSo… I don’t really know how often.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I suppose… what finally made me say something is that after I eat too much, I’ve been going upstairs and making myself throw up. And then I sort of come back down and act like I was just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed.\n\nIt feels awful saying that out loud. I kept thinking I could just manage it, you know, like how you keep on top of things at work—I'm an optician, so I’m used to being quite practical about problems—but this one’s got away from me a bit.\n\nDoctor: How often have you been making yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, really. It’s embarrassing to put a number on it… there’s hardly been a week since then where I haven’t had at least one of those private eating spells and then tried to undo it somehow.\n\nSometimes that’s making myself sick, yes. Other times I’ll skip meals the next day or go out walking far too much. I know you asked about the vomiting specifically—sorry, I do go round the houses—but it’s sort of all part of the same pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I am, though it sounds ridiculous when I say it. My daughter has told me I look too thin, and there’s a woman at church who said something similar—she meant it kindly, I think, though I ended up thinking about it all through the service instead of listening properly.\n\nBut even with that, if my skirt feels the slightest bit tight, or the scale is up even a pound, I just panic. It’s like my head tells me I’ve somehow become huge overnight, even though part of me knows that can’t be right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. Not every time ends with that, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes if the kitchen’s empty—after a church supper, for instance, when I’ve brought things home—I’ll start with just “putting away” leftovers, and then somehow I’ve eaten crackers and cheese, biscuits, bits of whatever I baked for book club… all in one go. Far more than I’d ever put on a plate and call a proper meal.\n\nIt’s strange, because I’m quite fussy about presentation normally—at work I’m measuring frames and noticing tiny details, and at home I’ll arrange things nicely if people are coming over—but when it happens it’s just standing there in the kitchen, picking at things until I realize how much has gone.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to undo your eating by making yourself throw up or skipping meals?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is. I wish I could say it was about feeling uncomfortable or wanting to be “healthy,” but really it’s the weight.\n\nEven when my daughter says I look too thin, or that woman from church mentioned it—she’s always so neat herself, you know, lovely hats on Sundays—still, if my skirt feels snug or the scale is up even a pound, I just… panic. It feels like I’ve suddenly let myself become huge overnight, even though some sensible part of me knows that can’t be true.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have these episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, really… I suppose there’s hardly been a week without it happening at least once.\n\nIt’s usually private, when no one’s about. I’ll have one of those spells where I eat far too much in a short time, and then I try to make up for it—sometimes by being sick, sometimes by not eating properly afterward, or by walking and walking more than I meant to. I don’t like admitting that.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have these episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without then making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, I think… there’s hardly been a week when I haven’t had at least one of those private spells. Not always exactly the same, you know—sometimes I tell myself I’m just tidying up food, the way my mother used to hate waste, and then it’s suddenly become something else.\n\nAnd afterward I usually try to make up for it somehow—being sick, or not eating properly, or walking far too much. I know you asked about without making myself throw up… but it all rather gets tangled together in my head.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to undo it by making yourself throw up, skipping meals, or excessive exercise?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out neatly, if I’m honest. In my head, the eating and the “making up for it” sort of belong together, even if it doesn’t always happen in the same way.\n\nThere may be times I’ve eaten too much and then just gone to bed or tried to carry on, but I don’t keep count. I’m sorry—my mind goes straight to whether I did something afterward, and then I get muddled. It’s like when I’m at work fitting someone’s frames and they ask one simple question, and suddenly I’m thinking about the whole shape of their face and the lighting in the shop… I lose the exact bit I was meant to answer.\n\nSo… I don’t really know how often.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you are too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, or at least I feel as if I do. My daughter has said I look too thin, and there’s a woman at church who said something similar—though she says things in that careful way people do over tea, you know, like she’s trying not to offend.\n\nBut if my skirt feels even a bit snug, or the scale is up by a pound, I just panic. It’s ridiculous, because part of me can hear what they’ve said, but another part of me feels as though I’ve become huge overnight, like I’ve let myself go completely.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I don’t really know, honestly. I don’t think of it as a separate thing very clearly.

Sometimes I’ll eat more than I meant to and then I suppose I don’t do anything obvious afterward… but then I might spend the whole evening thinking about it, or planning what I’ll do the next day, and it all blurs together. It’s a bit like at work when someone asks for one measurement and I start thinking about the bridge fit and the lens thickness and whether they’ll hate the frames in daylight—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.

So… I can’t give you a proper number. I haven’t kept track of the times when I don’t try to undo it.

I wish I could say it was about feeling uncomfortable or wanting to be “healthy,” but really it’s the weight.\n\nEven when my daughter says I look too thin, or that woman from church mentioned it—she’s always so neat herself, you know, lovely hats on Sundays—still, if my skirt feels snug or the scale is up even a pound, I just… panic. It feels like I’ve suddenly let myself become huge overnight, even though some sensible part of me knows that can’t be true.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have these episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, really… I suppose there’s hardly been a week without it happening at least once.\n\nIt’s usually private, when no one’s about. I’ll have one of those spells where I eat far too much in a short time, and then I try to make up for it—sometimes by being sick, sometimes by not eating properly afterward, or by walking and walking more than I meant to. I don’t like admitting that.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have these episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without then making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, I think… there’s hardly been a week when I haven’t had at least one of those private spells. Not always exactly the same, you know—sometimes I tell myself I’m just tidying up food, the way my mother used to hate waste, and then it’s suddenly become something else.\n\nAnd afterward I usually try to make up for it somehow—being sick, or not eating properly, or walking far too much. I know you asked about without making myself throw up… but it all rather gets tangled together in my head.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to undo it by making yourself throw up, skipping meals, or excessive exercise?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out neatly, if I’m honest. In my head, the eating and the “making up for it” sort of belong together, even if it doesn’t always happen in the same way.\n\nThere may be times I’ve eaten too much and then just gone to bed or tried to carry on, but I don’t keep count. I’m sorry—my mind goes straight to whether I did something afterward, and then I get muddled. It’s like when I’m at work fitting someone’s frames and they ask one simple question, and suddenly I’m thinking about the whole shape of their face and the lighting in the shop… I lose the exact bit I was meant to answer.\n\nSo… I don’t really know how often.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you are too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, or at least I feel as if I do. My daughter has said I look too thin, and there’s a woman at church who said something similar—though she says things in that careful way people do over tea, you know, like she’s trying not to offend.\n\nBut if my skirt feels even a bit snug, or the scale is up by a pound, I just panic. It’s ridiculous, because part of me can hear what they’ve said, but another part of me feels as though I’ve become huge overnight, like I’ve let myself go completely.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without doing anything to try to undo it?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, honestly. I don’t think of it as a separate thing very clearly.\n\nSometimes I’ll eat more than I meant to and then I suppose I don’t do anything obvious afterward… but then I might spend the whole evening thinking about it, or planning what I’ll do the next day, and it all blurs together. It’s a bit like at work when someone asks for one measurement and I start thinking about the bridge fit and the lens thickness and whether they’ll hate the frames in daylight—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… I can’t give you a proper number. I haven’t kept track of the times when I don’t try to undo it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It does happen… I mean, if no one’s in the kitchen, I can end up eating far more than I’d ever put on a plate for myself.

I wish I could say it was about feeling uncomfortable or wanting to be “healthy,” but really it’s the weight.\n\nEven when my daughter says I look too thin, or that woman from church mentioned it—she’s always so neat herself, you know, lovely hats on Sundays—still, if my skirt feels snug or the scale is up even a pound, I just… panic. It feels like I’ve suddenly let myself become huge overnight, even though some sensible part of me knows that can’t be true.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have these episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, really… I suppose there’s hardly been a week without it happening at least once.\n\nIt’s usually private, when no one’s about. I’ll have one of those spells where I eat far too much in a short time, and then I try to make up for it—sometimes by being sick, sometimes by not eating properly afterward, or by walking and walking more than I meant to. I don’t like admitting that.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have these episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without then making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, I think… there’s hardly been a week when I haven’t had at least one of those private spells. Not always exactly the same, you know—sometimes I tell myself I’m just tidying up food, the way my mother used to hate waste, and then it’s suddenly become something else.\n\nAnd afterward I usually try to make up for it somehow—being sick, or not eating properly, or walking far too much. I know you asked about without making myself throw up… but it all rather gets tangled together in my head.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to undo it by making yourself throw up, skipping meals, or excessive exercise?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out neatly, if I’m honest. In my head, the eating and the “making up for it” sort of belong together, even if it doesn’t always happen in the same way.\n\nThere may be times I’ve eaten too much and then just gone to bed or tried to carry on, but I don’t keep count. I’m sorry—my mind goes straight to whether I did something afterward, and then I get muddled. It’s like when I’m at work fitting someone’s frames and they ask one simple question, and suddenly I’m thinking about the whole shape of their face and the lighting in the shop… I lose the exact bit I was meant to answer.\n\nSo… I don’t really know how often.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you are too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, or at least I feel as if I do. My daughter has said I look too thin, and there’s a woman at church who said something similar—though she says things in that careful way people do over tea, you know, like she’s trying not to offend.\n\nBut if my skirt feels even a bit snug, or the scale is up by a pound, I just panic. It’s ridiculous, because part of me can hear what they’ve said, but another part of me feels as though I’ve become huge overnight, like I’ve let myself go completely.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without doing anything to try to undo it?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, honestly. I don’t think of it as a separate thing very clearly.\n\nSometimes I’ll eat more than I meant to and then I suppose I don’t do anything obvious afterward… but then I might spend the whole evening thinking about it, or planning what I’ll do the next day, and it all blurs together. It’s a bit like at work when someone asks for one measurement and I start thinking about the bridge fit and the lens thickness and whether they’ll hate the frames in daylight—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… I can’t give you a proper number. I haven’t kept track of the times when I don’t try to undo it.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: It does happen… I mean, if no one’s in the kitchen, I can end up eating far more than I’d ever put on a plate for myself.\n\nLike after a church supper, if there are leftovers, I’ll pick at them, and then it’s crackers, cheese, cookies, bits of whatever I baked for book club… and somehow it’s all in one sitting. It’s not like a normal meal. I’d be mortified to serve myself that much in front of someone.\n\nSorry, you asked how often—I’m not giving you a number. But yes, I do eat large amounts in a short time sometimes, especially when I’m alone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Not every time ends with that, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes if the kitchen’s empty—after a church supper, for instance, when I’ve brought things home—I’ll start with just “putting away” leftovers, and then somehow I’ve eaten crackers and cheese, biscuits, bits of whatever I baked for book club… all in one go. Far more than I’d ever put on a plate and call a proper meal.\n\nIt’s strange, because I’m quite fussy about presentation normally—at work I’m measuring frames and noticing tiny details, and at home I’ll arrange things nicely if people are coming over—but when it happens it’s just standing there in the kitchen, picking at things until I realize how much has gone.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to undo your eating by making yourself throw up or skipping meals?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is. I wish I could say it was about feeling uncomfortable or wanting to be “healthy,” but really it’s the weight.\n\nEven when my daughter says I look too thin, or that woman from church mentioned it—she’s always so neat herself, you know, lovely hats on Sundays—still, if my skirt feels snug or the scale is up even a pound, I just… panic. It feels like I’ve suddenly let myself become huge overnight, even though some sensible part of me knows that can’t be true.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have these episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, really… I suppose there’s hardly been a week without it happening at least once.\n\nIt’s usually private, when no one’s about. I’ll have one of those spells where I eat far too much in a short time, and then I try to make up for it—sometimes by being sick, sometimes by not eating properly afterward, or by walking and walking more than I meant to. I don’t like admitting that.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have these episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without then making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, I think… there’s hardly been a week when I haven’t had at least one of those private spells. Not always exactly the same, you know—sometimes I tell myself I’m just tidying up food, the way my mother used to hate waste, and then it’s suddenly become something else.\n\nAnd afterward I usually try to make up for it somehow—being sick, or not eating properly, or walking far too much. I know you asked about without making myself throw up… but it all rather gets tangled together in my head.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to undo it by making yourself throw up, skipping meals, or excessive exercise?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out neatly, if I’m honest. In my head, the eating and the “making up for it” sort of belong together, even if it doesn’t always happen in the same way.\n\nThere may be times I’ve eaten too much and then just gone to bed or tried to carry on, but I don’t keep count. I’m sorry—my mind goes straight to whether I did something afterward, and then I get muddled. It’s like when I’m at work fitting someone’s frames and they ask one simple question, and suddenly I’m thinking about the whole shape of their face and the lighting in the shop… I lose the exact bit I was meant to answer.\n\nSo… I don’t really know how often.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you are too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, or at least I feel as if I do. My daughter has said I look too thin, and there’s a woman at church who said something similar—though she says things in that careful way people do over tea, you know, like she’s trying not to offend.\n\nBut if my skirt feels even a bit snug, or the scale is up by a pound, I just panic. It’s ridiculous, because part of me can hear what they’ve said, but another part of me feels as though I’ve become huge overnight, like I’ve let myself go completely.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without doing anything to try to undo it?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, honestly. I don’t think of it as a separate thing very clearly.\n\nSometimes I’ll eat more than I meant to and then I suppose I don’t do anything obvious afterward… but then I might spend the whole evening thinking about it, or planning what I’ll do the next day, and it all blurs together. It’s a bit like at work when someone asks for one measurement and I start thinking about the bridge fit and the lens thickness and whether they’ll hate the frames in daylight—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… I can’t give you a proper number. I haven’t kept track of the times when I don’t try to undo it.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: It does happen… I mean, if no one’s in the kitchen, I can end up eating far more than I’d ever put on a plate for myself.\n\nLike after a church supper, if there are leftovers, I’ll pick at them, and then it’s crackers, cheese, cookies, bits of whatever I baked for book club… and somehow it’s all in one sitting. It’s not like a normal meal. I’d be mortified to serve myself that much in front of someone.\n\nSorry, you asked how often—I’m not giving you a number. But yes, I do eat large amounts in a short time sometimes, especially when I’m alone.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without then trying to make up for it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it cleanly like that. Sometimes I eat too much and then I tell myself I’ll be sensible the next day, but I don’t know whether that counts as “making up for it” or not.\n\nI haven’t really been keeping a tally. It’s a bit like when people come into the shop saying their glasses are “not right,” and you have to work out if it’s the prescription, the frame sitting crooked, the lighting—sorry, that’s not helpful.\n\nI suppose there are times when I don’t do anything afterward, but how often… I honestly don’t know.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "content": "Return the JSON now."

From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I suppose… what finally made me say something is that after I eat too much, I’ve been going upstairs and making myself throw up. And then I sort of come back down and act like I was just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed.\n\nIt feels awful saying that out loud. I kept thinking I could just manage it, you know, like how you keep on top of things at work—I'm an optician, so I’m used to being quite practical about problems—but this one’s got away from me a bit.\n\nDoctor: How often have you been making yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, really. It’s embarrassing to put a number on it… there’s hardly been a week since then where I haven’t had at least one of those private eating spells and then tried to undo it somehow.\n\nSometimes that’s making myself sick, yes. Other times I’ll skip meals the next day or go out walking far too much. I know you asked about the vomiting specifically—sorry, I do go round the houses—but it’s sort of all part of the same pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I am, though it sounds ridiculous when I say it. My daughter has told me I look too thin, and there’s a woman at church who said something similar—she meant it kindly, I think, though I ended up thinking about it all through the service instead of listening properly.\n\nBut even with that, if my skirt feels the slightest bit tight, or the scale is up even a pound, I just panic. It’s like my head tells me I’ve somehow become huge overnight, even though part of me knows that can’t be right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. Not every time ends with that, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes if the kitchen’s empty—after a church supper, for instance, when I’ve brought things home—I’ll start with just “putting away” leftovers, and then somehow I’ve eaten crackers and cheese, biscuits, bits of whatever I baked for book club… all in one go. Far more than I’d ever put on a plate and call a proper meal.\n\nIt’s strange, because I’m quite fussy about presentation normally—at work I’m measuring frames and noticing tiny details, and at home I’ll arrange things nicely if people are coming over—but when it happens it’s just standing there in the kitchen, picking at things until I realize how much has gone.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to undo your eating by making yourself throw up or skipping meals?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is. I wish I could say it was about feeling uncomfortable or wanting to be “healthy,” but really it’s the weight.\n\nEven when my daughter says I look too thin, or that woman from church mentioned it—she’s always so neat herself, you know, lovely hats on Sundays—still, if my skirt feels snug or the scale is up even a pound, I just… panic. It feels like I’ve suddenly let myself become huge overnight, even though some sensible part of me knows that can’t be true.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have these episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, really… I suppose there’s hardly been a week without it happening at least once.\n\nIt’s usually private, when no one’s about. I’ll have one of those spells where I eat far too much in a short time, and then I try to make up for it—sometimes by being sick, sometimes by not eating properly afterward, or by walking and walking more than I meant to. I don’t like admitting that.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have these episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without then making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, I think… there’s hardly been a week when I haven’t had at least one of those private spells. Not always exactly the same, you know—sometimes I tell myself I’m just tidying up food, the way my mother used to hate waste, and then it’s suddenly become something else.\n\nAnd afterward I usually try to make up for it somehow—being sick, or not eating properly, or walking far too much. I know you asked about without making myself throw up… but it all rather gets tangled together in my head.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to undo it by making yourself throw up, skipping meals, or excessive exercise?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out neatly, if I’m honest. In my head, the eating and the “making up for it” sort of belong together, even if it doesn’t always happen in the same way.\n\nThere may be times I’ve eaten too much and then just gone to bed or tried to carry on, but I don’t keep count. I’m sorry—my mind goes straight to whether I did something afterward, and then I get muddled. It’s like when I’m at work fitting someone’s frames and they ask one simple question, and suddenly I’m thinking about the whole shape of their face and the lighting in the shop… I lose the exact bit I was meant to answer.\n\nSo… I don’t really know how often.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you are too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, or at least I feel as if I do. My daughter has said I look too thin, and there’s a woman at church who said something similar—though she says things in that careful way people do over tea, you know, like she’s trying not to offend.\n\nBut if my skirt feels even a bit snug, or the scale is up by a pound, I just panic. It’s ridiculous, because part of me can hear what they’ve said, but another part of me feels as though I’ve become huge overnight, like I’ve let myself go completely.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without doing anything to try to undo it?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, honestly. I don’t think of it as a separate thing very clearly.\n\nSometimes I’ll eat more than I meant to and then I suppose I don’t do anything obvious afterward… but then I might spend the whole evening thinking about it, or planning what I’ll do the next day, and it all blurs together. It’s a bit like at work when someone asks for one measurement and I start thinking about the bridge fit and the lens thickness and whether they’ll hate the frames in daylight—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… I can’t give you a proper number. I haven’t kept track of the times when I don’t try to undo it.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: It does happen… I mean, if no one’s in the kitchen, I can end up eating far more than I’d ever put on a plate for myself.\n\nLike after a church supper, if there are leftovers, I’ll pick at them, and then it’s crackers, cheese, cookies, bits of whatever I baked for book club… and somehow it’s all in one sitting. It’s not like a normal meal. I’d be mortified to serve myself that much in front of someone.\n\nSorry, you asked how often—I’m not giving you a number. But yes, I do eat large amounts in a short time sometimes, especially when I’m alone.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without then trying to make up for it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it cleanly like that. Sometimes I eat too much and then I tell myself I’ll be sensible the next day, but I don’t know whether that counts as “making up for it” or not.\n\nI haven’t really been keeping a tally. It’s a bit like when people come into the shop saying their glasses are “not right,” and you have to work out if it’s the prescription, the frame sitting crooked, the lighting—sorry, that’s not helpful.\n\nI suppose there are times when I don’t do anything afterward, but how often… I honestly don’t know.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without doing anything to try to undo it afterwards?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know how to count that. I know that probably sounds evasive, but it isn’t meant to be.\n\nIf I’ve eaten a lot in one go, even if I don’t do anything obvious afterward, I may still spend ages thinking about it or making little promises to myself about later. So I’m not sure what counts as “undoing it” and what doesn’t.\n\nI haven’t kept track of the times where I just… leave it alone afterward. I’m sorry, I can’t give you a proper frequency.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It’s ridiculous, because part of me can hear what they’ve said, but another part of me feels as though I’ve become huge overnight, like I’ve let myself go completely.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without doing anything to try to undo it?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, honestly. I’m sorry, I know you’ve asked it very clearly and I’m still not giving you a neat answer.

I know you asked about without making myself throw up… but it all rather gets tangled together in my head.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to undo it by making yourself throw up, skipping meals, or excessive exercise?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out neatly, if I’m honest. In my head, the eating and the “making up for it” sort of belong together, even if it doesn’t always happen in the same way.\n\nThere may be times I’ve eaten too much and then just gone to bed or tried to carry on, but I don’t keep count. I’m sorry—my mind goes straight to whether I did something afterward, and then I get muddled. It’s like when I’m at work fitting someone’s frames and they ask one simple question, and suddenly I’m thinking about the whole shape of their face and the lighting in the shop… I lose the exact bit I was meant to answer.\n\nSo… I don’t really know how often.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you are too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, or at least I feel as if I do. My daughter has said I look too thin, and there’s a woman at church who said something similar—though she says things in that careful way people do over tea, you know, like she’s trying not to offend.\n\nBut if my skirt feels even a bit snug, or the scale is up by a pound, I just panic. It’s ridiculous, because part of me can hear what they’ve said, but another part of me feels as though I’ve become huge overnight, like I’ve let myself go completely.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without doing anything to try to undo it?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, honestly. I don’t think of it as a separate thing very clearly.\n\nSometimes I’ll eat more than I meant to and then I suppose I don’t do anything obvious afterward… but then I might spend the whole evening thinking about it, or planning what I’ll do the next day, and it all blurs together. It’s a bit like at work when someone asks for one measurement and I start thinking about the bridge fit and the lens thickness and whether they’ll hate the frames in daylight—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… I can’t give you a proper number. I haven’t kept track of the times when I don’t try to undo it.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: It does happen… I mean, if no one’s in the kitchen, I can end up eating far more than I’d ever put on a plate for myself.\n\nLike after a church supper, if there are leftovers, I’ll pick at them, and then it’s crackers, cheese, cookies, bits of whatever I baked for book club… and somehow it’s all in one sitting. It’s not like a normal meal. I’d be mortified to serve myself that much in front of someone.\n\nSorry, you asked how often—I’m not giving you a number. But yes, I do eat large amounts in a short time sometimes, especially when I’m alone.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without then trying to make up for it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it cleanly like that. Sometimes I eat too much and then I tell myself I’ll be sensible the next day, but I don’t know whether that counts as “making up for it” or not.\n\nI haven’t really been keeping a tally. It’s a bit like when people come into the shop saying their glasses are “not right,” and you have to work out if it’s the prescription, the frame sitting crooked, the lighting—sorry, that’s not helpful.\n\nI suppose there are times when I don’t do anything afterward, but how often… I honestly don’t know.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without doing anything to try to undo it afterwards?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know how to count that. I know that probably sounds evasive, but it isn’t meant to be.\n\nIf I’ve eaten a lot in one go, even if I don’t do anything obvious afterward, I may still spend ages thinking about it or making little promises to myself about later. So I’m not sure what counts as “undoing it” and what doesn’t.\n\nI haven’t kept track of the times where I just… leave it alone afterward. I’m sorry, I can’t give you a proper frequency.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. My daughter has said I look too thin, and a woman from church mentioned it too—though people at church do comment on everything, don’t they, the flowers, the hymns, who made the sponge cake—\n\nBut even when they say that, I don’t really see it that way. If my skirt feels tight, or the scale is up even one pound, I panic. It feels like I’ve suddenly become enormous overnight, even if other people are telling me the opposite.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without doing anything to try to undo it afterwards, such as making yourself throw up, skipping meals, or exercising excessively?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’ve asked it very clearly and I’m still not giving you a neat answer.\n\nI don’t really know how often. I don’t keep track of the ones where I just… don’t do anything. Yes… I mean, it does happen. If no one’s in the kitchen, I can start picking at leftovers from a church supper, then it’s crackers and cheese, then biscuits or cookies, and sometimes whatever I’d baked for book club.

It’s not like a normal plate of dinner. It’s much more than I’d ever put out for myself as a meal. I sort of tell myself I’m just tidying things away, which is ridiculous really, because tidying doesn’t usually involve eating half the kitchen.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I suppose… what finally made me say something is that after I eat too much, I’ve been going upstairs and making myself throw up. And then I sort of come back down and act like I was just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed.\n\nIt feels awful saying that out loud. I kept thinking I could just manage it, you know, like how you keep on top of things at work—I'm an optician, so I’m used to being quite practical about problems—but this one’s got away from me a bit.\n\nDoctor: How often have you been making yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, really. It’s embarrassing to put a number on it… there’s hardly been a week since then where I haven’t had at least one of those private eating spells and then tried to undo it somehow.\n\nSometimes that’s making myself sick, yes. Other times I’ll skip meals the next day or go out walking far too much. I know you asked about the vomiting specifically—sorry, I do go round the houses—but it’s sort of all part of the same pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I am, though it sounds ridiculous when I say it. My daughter has told me I look too thin, and there’s a woman at church who said something similar—she meant it kindly, I think, though I ended up thinking about it all through the service instead of listening properly.\n\nBut even with that, if my skirt feels the slightest bit tight, or the scale is up even a pound, I just panic. It’s like my head tells me I’ve somehow become huge overnight, even though part of me knows that can’t be right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. Not every time ends with that, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes if the kitchen’s empty—after a church supper, for instance, when I’ve brought things home—I’ll start with just “putting away” leftovers, and then somehow I’ve eaten crackers and cheese, biscuits, bits of whatever I baked for book club… all in one go. Far more than I’d ever put on a plate and call a proper meal.\n\nIt’s strange, because I’m quite fussy about presentation normally—at work I’m measuring frames and noticing tiny details, and at home I’ll arrange things nicely if people are coming over—but when it happens it’s just standing there in the kitchen, picking at things until I realize how much has gone.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to undo your eating by making yourself throw up or skipping meals?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is. I wish I could say it was about feeling uncomfortable or wanting to be “healthy,” but really it’s the weight.\n\nEven when my daughter says I look too thin, or that woman from church mentioned it—she’s always so neat herself, you know, lovely hats on Sundays—still, if my skirt feels snug or the scale is up even a pound, I just… panic. It feels like I’ve suddenly let myself become huge overnight, even though some sensible part of me knows that can’t be true.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have these episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, really… I suppose there’s hardly been a week without it happening at least once.\n\nIt’s usually private, when no one’s about. I’ll have one of those spells where I eat far too much in a short time, and then I try to make up for it—sometimes by being sick, sometimes by not eating properly afterward, or by walking and walking more than I meant to. I don’t like admitting that.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have these episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without then making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, I think… there’s hardly been a week when I haven’t had at least one of those private spells. Not always exactly the same, you know—sometimes I tell myself I’m just tidying up food, the way my mother used to hate waste, and then it’s suddenly become something else.\n\nAnd afterward I usually try to make up for it somehow—being sick, or not eating properly, or walking far too much. I know you asked about without making myself throw up… but it all rather gets tangled together in my head.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to undo it by making yourself throw up, skipping meals, or excessive exercise?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out neatly, if I’m honest. In my head, the eating and the “making up for it” sort of belong together, even if it doesn’t always happen in the same way.\n\nThere may be times I’ve eaten too much and then just gone to bed or tried to carry on, but I don’t keep count. I’m sorry—my mind goes straight to whether I did something afterward, and then I get muddled. It’s like when I’m at work fitting someone’s frames and they ask one simple question, and suddenly I’m thinking about the whole shape of their face and the lighting in the shop… I lose the exact bit I was meant to answer.\n\nSo… I don’t really know how often.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you are too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, or at least I feel as if I do. My daughter has said I look too thin, and there’s a woman at church who said something similar—though she says things in that careful way people do over tea, you know, like she’s trying not to offend.\n\nBut if my skirt feels even a bit snug, or the scale is up by a pound, I just panic. It’s ridiculous, because part of me can hear what they’ve said, but another part of me feels as though I’ve become huge overnight, like I’ve let myself go completely.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without doing anything to try to undo it?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, honestly. I don’t think of it as a separate thing very clearly.\n\nSometimes I’ll eat more than I meant to and then I suppose I don’t do anything obvious afterward… but then I might spend the whole evening thinking about it, or planning what I’ll do the next day, and it all blurs together. It’s a bit like at work when someone asks for one measurement and I start thinking about the bridge fit and the lens thickness and whether they’ll hate the frames in daylight—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… I can’t give you a proper number. I haven’t kept track of the times when I don’t try to undo it.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: It does happen… I mean, if no one’s in the kitchen, I can end up eating far more than I’d ever put on a plate for myself.\n\nLike after a church supper, if there are leftovers, I’ll pick at them, and then it’s crackers, cheese, cookies, bits of whatever I baked for book club… and somehow it’s all in one sitting. It’s not like a normal meal. I’d be mortified to serve myself that much in front of someone.\n\nSorry, you asked how often—I’m not giving you a number. But yes, I do eat large amounts in a short time sometimes, especially when I’m alone.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without then trying to make up for it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it cleanly like that. Sometimes I eat too much and then I tell myself I’ll be sensible the next day, but I don’t know whether that counts as “making up for it” or not.\n\nI haven’t really been keeping a tally. I know that probably sounds evasive, but it isn’t meant to be.\n\nIf I’ve eaten a lot in one go, even if I don’t do anything obvious afterward, I may still spend ages thinking about it or making little promises to myself about later. So I’m not sure what counts as “undoing it” and what doesn’t.\n\nI haven’t kept track of the times where I just… leave it alone afterward. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really know how to answer that properly. I mean, sometimes I suppose I’m just… tired, and I don’t do anything obvious afterwards, but then my mind is still going round and round about it.

And “large amount” is hard for me to judge, because if it’s just bits here and there it doesn’t look like a meal, does it? Like when you’re putting away leftovers after an event and there are three different plates and someone’s wrapped things badly in cling film—sorry, that’s not the point.

I can’t honestly tell you how often that happens, or even if I’d count it the way you mean.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I suppose… what finally made me say something is that after I eat too much, I’ve been going upstairs and making myself throw up. And then I sort of come back down and act like I was just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed.\n\nIt feels awful saying that out loud. I kept thinking I could just manage it, you know, like how you keep on top of things at work—I'm an optician, so I’m used to being quite practical about problems—but this one’s got away from me a bit.\n\nDoctor: How often have you been making yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, really. It’s embarrassing to put a number on it… there’s hardly been a week since then where I haven’t had at least one of those private eating spells and then tried to undo it somehow.\n\nSometimes that’s making myself sick, yes. Other times I’ll skip meals the next day or go out walking far too much. I know you asked about the vomiting specifically—sorry, I do go round the houses—but it’s sort of all part of the same pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I am, though it sounds ridiculous when I say it. My daughter has told me I look too thin, and there’s a woman at church who said something similar—she meant it kindly, I think, though I ended up thinking about it all through the service instead of listening properly.\n\nBut even with that, if my skirt feels the slightest bit tight, or the scale is up even a pound, I just panic. It’s like my head tells me I’ve somehow become huge overnight, even though part of me knows that can’t be right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. Not every time ends with that, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes if the kitchen’s empty—after a church supper, for instance, when I’ve brought things home—I’ll start with just “putting away” leftovers, and then somehow I’ve eaten crackers and cheese, biscuits, bits of whatever I baked for book club… all in one go. Far more than I’d ever put on a plate and call a proper meal.\n\nIt’s strange, because I’m quite fussy about presentation normally—at work I’m measuring frames and noticing tiny details, and at home I’ll arrange things nicely if people are coming over—but when it happens it’s just standing there in the kitchen, picking at things until I realize how much has gone.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to undo your eating by making yourself throw up or skipping meals?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is. I wish I could say it was about feeling uncomfortable or wanting to be “healthy,” but really it’s the weight.\n\nEven when my daughter says I look too thin, or that woman from church mentioned it—she’s always so neat herself, you know, lovely hats on Sundays—still, if my skirt feels snug or the scale is up even a pound, I just… panic. It feels like I’ve suddenly let myself become huge overnight, even though some sensible part of me knows that can’t be true.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have these episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, really… I suppose there’s hardly been a week without it happening at least once.\n\nIt’s usually private, when no one’s about. I’ll have one of those spells where I eat far too much in a short time, and then I try to make up for it—sometimes by being sick, sometimes by not eating properly afterward, or by walking and walking more than I meant to. I don’t like admitting that.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have these episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without then making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, I think… there’s hardly been a week when I haven’t had at least one of those private spells. Not always exactly the same, you know—sometimes I tell myself I’m just tidying up food, the way my mother used to hate waste, and then it’s suddenly become something else.\n\nAnd afterward I usually try to make up for it somehow—being sick, or not eating properly, or walking far too much. I know you asked about without making myself throw up… but it all rather gets tangled together in my head.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to undo it by making yourself throw up, skipping meals, or excessive exercise?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out neatly, if I’m honest. In my head, the eating and the “making up for it” sort of belong together, even if it doesn’t always happen in the same way.\n\nThere may be times I’ve eaten too much and then just gone to bed or tried to carry on, but I don’t keep count. I’m sorry—my mind goes straight to whether I did something afterward, and then I get muddled. It’s like when I’m at work fitting someone’s frames and they ask one simple question, and suddenly I’m thinking about the whole shape of their face and the lighting in the shop… I lose the exact bit I was meant to answer.\n\nSo… I don’t really know how often.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you are too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, or at least I feel as if I do. My daughter has said I look too thin, and there’s a woman at church who said something similar—though she says things in that careful way people do over tea, you know, like she’s trying not to offend.\n\nBut if my skirt feels even a bit snug, or the scale is up by a pound, I just panic. It’s ridiculous, because part of me can hear what they’ve said, but another part of me feels as though I’ve become huge overnight, like I’ve let myself go completely.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without doing anything to try to undo it?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, honestly. I don’t think of it as a separate thing very clearly.\n\nSometimes I’ll eat more than I meant to and then I suppose I don’t do anything obvious afterward… but then I might spend the whole evening thinking about it, or planning what I’ll do the next day, and it all blurs together. It’s a bit like at work when someone asks for one measurement and I start thinking about the bridge fit and the lens thickness and whether they’ll hate the frames in daylight—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… I can’t give you a proper number. I haven’t kept track of the times when I don’t try to undo it.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: It does happen… I mean, if no one’s in the kitchen, I can end up eating far more than I’d ever put on a plate for myself.\n\nLike after a church supper, if there are leftovers, I’ll pick at them, and then it’s crackers, cheese, cookies, bits of whatever I baked for book club… and somehow it’s all in one sitting. It’s not like a normal meal. I’d be mortified to serve myself that much in front of someone.\n\nSorry, you asked how often—I’m not giving you a number. But yes, I do eat large amounts in a short time sometimes, especially when I’m alone.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without then trying to make up for it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it cleanly like that. Sometimes I eat too much and then I tell myself I’ll be sensible the next day, but I don’t know whether that counts as “making up for it” or not.\n\nI haven’t really been keeping a tally. It’s a bit like when people come into the shop saying their glasses are “not right,” and you have to work out if it’s the prescription, the frame sitting crooked, the lighting—sorry, that’s not helpful.\n\nI suppose there are times when I don’t do anything afterward, but how often… I honestly don’t know.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without doing anything to try to undo it afterwards?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know how to count that. I know that probably sounds evasive, but it isn’t meant to be.\n\nIf I’ve eaten a lot in one go, even if I don’t do anything obvious afterward, I may still spend ages thinking about it or making little promises to myself about later. So I’m not sure what counts as “undoing it” and what doesn’t.\n\nI haven’t kept track of the times where I just… leave it alone afterward. I’m sorry, I can’t give you a proper frequency.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. My daughter has said I look too thin, and a woman from church mentioned it too—though people at church do comment on everything, don’t they, the flowers, the hymns, who made the sponge cake—\n\nBut even when they say that, I don’t really see it that way. If my skirt feels tight, or the scale is up even one pound, I panic. It feels like I’ve suddenly become enormous overnight, even if other people are telling me the opposite.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without doing anything to try to undo it afterwards, such as making yourself throw up, skipping meals, or exercising excessively?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’ve asked it very clearly and I’m still not giving you a neat answer.\n\nI don’t really know how often. I don’t keep track of the ones where I just… don’t do anything. And sometimes I can’t tell whether I’ve “done something” or not, because I might not do anything obvious, but then I’m thinking about it all evening, or deciding I’ll be different tomorrow.\n\nIt’s like at work when someone says their glasses are slipping, and it could be the bridge, the arms, the way they put them on—sorry, I’m wandering. I just mean I can’t sort it into a number for you.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it does happen. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Yes… I think it’s tied up in that. Not in a sensible way, but it is.

It’s like my head tells me I’ve somehow become huge overnight, even though part of me knows that can’t be right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. Not always exactly the same, you know—sometimes I tell myself I’m just tidying up food, the way my mother used to hate waste, and then it’s suddenly become something else.\n\nAnd afterward I usually try to make up for it somehow—being sick, or not eating properly, or walking far too much. I know you asked about without making myself throw up… but it all rather gets tangled together in my head.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to undo it by making yourself throw up, skipping meals, or excessive exercise?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out neatly, if I’m honest. I’m sorry—my mind goes straight to whether I did something afterward, and then I get muddled. My daughter has said I look too thin, and there’s a woman at church who said something similar—though she says things in that careful way people do over tea, you know, like she’s trying not to offend.\n\nBut if my skirt feels even a bit snug, or the scale is up by a pound, I just panic. It’s ridiculous, because part of me can hear what they’ve said, but another part of me feels as though I’ve become huge overnight, like I’ve let myself go completely.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without doing anything to try to undo it?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, honestly. I don’t think of it as a separate thing very clearly.\n\nSometimes I’ll eat more than I meant to and then I suppose I don’t do anything obvious afterward… but then I might spend the whole evening thinking about it, or planning what I’ll do the next day, and it all blurs together. It’s a bit like at work when someone asks for one measurement and I start thinking about the bridge fit and the lens thickness and whether they’ll hate the frames in daylight—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… I can’t give you a proper number. I haven’t kept track of the times when I don’t try to undo it.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: It does happen… I mean, if no one’s in the kitchen, I can end up eating far more than I’d ever put on a plate for myself.\n\nLike after a church supper, if there are leftovers, I’ll pick at them, and then it’s crackers, cheese, cookies, bits of whatever I baked for book club… and somehow it’s all in one sitting. Far more than I’d ever put on a plate and call a proper meal.\n\nIt’s strange, because I’m quite fussy about presentation normally—at work I’m measuring frames and noticing tiny details, and at home I’ll arrange things nicely if people are coming over—but when it happens it’s just standing there in the kitchen, picking at things until I realize how much has gone.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to undo your eating by making yourself throw up or skipping meals?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is. I wish I could say it was about feeling uncomfortable or wanting to be “healthy,” but really it’s the weight.\n\nEven when my daughter says I look too thin, or that woman from church mentioned it—she’s always so neat herself, you know, lovely hats on Sundays—still, if my skirt feels snug or the scale is up even a pound, I just… panic. It feels like I’ve suddenly let myself become huge overnight, even though some sensible part of me knows that can’t be true.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have these episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, really… I suppose there’s hardly been a week without it happening at least once.\n\nIt’s usually private, when no one’s about. I’ll have one of those spells where I eat far too much in a short time, and then I try to make up for it—sometimes by being sick, sometimes by not eating properly afterward, or by walking and walking more than I meant to. I don’t like admitting that.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have these episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without then making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, I think… there’s hardly been a week when I haven’t had at least one of those private spells. Not always exactly the same, you know—sometimes I tell myself I’m just tidying up food, the way my mother used to hate waste, and then it’s suddenly become something else.\n\nAnd afterward I usually try to make up for it somehow—being sick, or not eating properly, or walking far too much. I know you asked about without making myself throw up… but it all rather gets tangled together in my head.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to undo it by making yourself throw up, skipping meals, or excessive exercise?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out neatly, if I’m honest. In my head, the eating and the “making up for it” sort of belong together, even if it doesn’t always happen in the same way.\n\nThere may be times I’ve eaten too much and then just gone to bed or tried to carry on, but I don’t keep count. I’m sorry—my mind goes straight to whether I did something afterward, and then I get muddled. It’s like when I’m at work fitting someone’s frames and they ask one simple question, and suddenly I’m thinking about the whole shape of their face and the lighting in the shop… I lose the exact bit I was meant to answer.\n\nSo… I don’t really know how often.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you are too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, or at least I feel as if I do. My daughter has said I look too thin, and there’s a woman at church who said something similar—though she says things in that careful way people do over tea, you know, like she’s trying not to offend.\n\nBut if my skirt feels even a bit snug, or the scale is up by a pound, I just panic. It’s ridiculous, because part of me can hear what they’ve said, but another part of me feels as though I’ve become huge overnight, like I’ve let myself go completely.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without doing anything to try to undo it?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, honestly. I don’t think of it as a separate thing very clearly.\n\nSometimes I’ll eat more than I meant to and then I suppose I don’t do anything obvious afterward… but then I might spend the whole evening thinking about it, or planning what I’ll do the next day, and it all blurs together. It’s a bit like at work when someone asks for one measurement and I start thinking about the bridge fit and the lens thickness and whether they’ll hate the frames in daylight—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… I can’t give you a proper number. I haven’t kept track of the times when I don’t try to undo it.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: It does happen… I mean, if no one’s in the kitchen, I can end up eating far more than I’d ever put on a plate for myself.\n\nLike after a church supper, if there are leftovers, I’ll pick at them, and then it’s crackers, cheese, cookies, bits of whatever I baked for book club… and somehow it’s all in one sitting. It’s not like a normal meal. I’d be mortified to serve myself that much in front of someone.\n\nSorry, you asked how often—I’m not giving you a number. But yes, I do eat large amounts in a short time sometimes, especially when I’m alone.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without then trying to make up for it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it cleanly like that. Sometimes I eat too much and then I tell myself I’ll be sensible the next day, but I don’t know whether that counts as “making up for it” or not.\n\nI haven’t really been keeping a tally. It’s a bit like when people come into the shop saying their glasses are “not right,” and you have to work out if it’s the prescription, the frame sitting crooked, the lighting—sorry, that’s not helpful.\n\nI suppose there are times when I don’t do anything afterward, but how often… I honestly don’t know.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without doing anything to try to undo it afterwards?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know how to count that. I know that probably sounds evasive, but it isn’t meant to be.\n\nIf I’ve eaten a lot in one go, even if I don’t do anything obvious afterward, I may still spend ages thinking about it or making little promises to myself about later. So I’m not sure what counts as “undoing it” and what doesn’t.\n\nI haven’t kept track of the times where I just… leave it alone afterward. I’m sorry, I can’t give you a proper frequency.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. My daughter has said I look too thin, and a woman from church mentioned it too—though people at church do comment on everything, don’t they, the flowers, the hymns, who made the sponge cake—\n\nBut even when they say that, I don’t really see it that way. If my skirt feels tight, or the scale is up even one pound, I panic. It feels like I’ve suddenly become enormous overnight, even if other people are telling me the opposite.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without doing anything to try to undo it afterwards, such as making yourself throw up, skipping meals, or exercising excessively?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’ve asked it very clearly and I’m still not giving you a neat answer.\n\nI don’t really know how often. I don’t keep track of the ones where I just… don’t do anything. And sometimes I can’t tell whether I’ve “done something” or not, because I might not do anything obvious, but then I’m thinking about it all evening, or deciding I’ll be different tomorrow.\n\nIt’s like at work when someone says their glasses are slipping, and it could be the bridge, the arms, the way they put them on—sorry, I’m wandering. I just mean I can’t sort it into a number for you.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it does happen. If no one’s in the kitchen, I can start picking at leftovers from a church supper, then it’s crackers and cheese, then biscuits or cookies, and sometimes whatever I’d baked for book club.\n\nIt’s not like a normal plate of dinner. It’s much more than I’d ever put out for myself as a meal. I sort of tell myself I’m just tidying things away, which is ridiculous really, because tidying doesn’t usually involve eating half the kitchen.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time and then not try to undo it in any way?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that properly. I mean, sometimes I suppose I’m just… tired, and I don’t do anything obvious afterwards, but then my mind is still going round and round about it.\n\nAnd “large amount” is hard for me to judge, because if it’s just bits here and there it doesn’t look like a meal, does it? Like when you’re putting away leftovers after an event and there are three different plates and someone’s wrapped things badly in cling film—sorry, that’s not the point.\n\nI can’t honestly tell you how often that happens, or even if I’d count it the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the reason you eat large amounts of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it’s tied up in that. I haven’t kept track of the times when I don’t try to undo it.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: It does happen… I mean, if no one’s in the kitchen, I can end up eating far more than I’d ever put on a plate for myself.\n\nLike after a church supper, if there are leftovers, I’ll pick at them, and then it’s crackers, cheese, cookies, bits of whatever I baked for book club… and somehow it’s all in one sitting. It’s not like a normal meal. I’d be mortified to serve myself that much in front of someone.\n\nSorry, you asked how often—I’m not giving you a number. But yes, I do eat large amounts in a short time sometimes, especially when I’m alone.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without then trying to make up for it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it cleanly like that. Sometimes I eat too much and then I tell myself I’ll be sensible the next day, but I don’t know whether that counts as “making up for it” or not.\n\nI haven’t really been keeping a tally. It’s a bit like when people come into the shop saying their glasses are “not right,” and you have to work out if it’s the prescription, the frame sitting crooked, the lighting—sorry, that’s not helpful.\n\nI suppose there are times when I don’t do anything afterward, but how often… I honestly don’t know.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without doing anything to try to undo it afterwards?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know how to count that. I know that probably sounds evasive, but it isn’t meant to be.\n\nIf I’ve eaten a lot in one go, even if I don’t do anything obvious afterward, I may still spend ages thinking about it or making little promises to myself about later. So I’m not sure what counts as “undoing it” and what doesn’t.\n\nI haven’t kept track of the times where I just… leave it alone afterward. I’m sorry, I can’t give you a proper frequency.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. My daughter has said I look too thin, and a woman from church mentioned it too—though people at church do comment on everything, don’t they, the flowers, the hymns, who made the sponge cake—\n\nBut even when they say that, I don’t really see it that way. If my skirt feels tight, or the scale is up even one pound, I panic. It feels like I’ve suddenly become enormous overnight, even if other people are telling me the opposite.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without doing anything to try to undo it afterwards, such as making yourself throw up, skipping meals, or exercising excessively?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’ve asked it very clearly and I’m still not giving you a neat answer.\n\nI don’t really know how often. I don’t keep track of the ones where I just… don’t do anything. It’s much more than I’d ever put out for myself as a meal. I sort of tell myself I’m just tidying things away, which is ridiculous really, because tidying doesn’t usually involve eating half the kitchen.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time and then not try to undo it in any way?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that properly.
